Exploring faster stride 0 min and max loops for plans/min-max.md

Claude · Oct 6, 2026 · 19 prompts · 33 replies · #133

Oct 6, 2026, 18:23 UTC

Davis

I think that RRAY REDUCE has a possible optimization in the out stride 0 case. Currently it rewrites to out on every iteration, but in other files we write into a local and only write into out at the end of each loop.

If you agree, create feature/reduce-write branch and propose a fix for this

Benchmark it against all reducers that use this macro and report back

Claude

src/reduce.h:47:#define RRAY_REDUCE(                                                           \
#ifndef RRAY_REDUCE_H
#define RRAY_REDUCE_H

#include "rlang.h"

#include "arg.h"
#include "strided-iterator.h"

// --------------------------------------------------------------------------
// rray_reduce

// Flat reduction is useful when the output element is the only state carried
// between input elements. It walks `x` once in storage order, updating the
// output element that each input contributes to. This is faster than nested
// reduction because it reads `x` linearly and avoids a separate inner traversal
// for each output element.

typedef r_obj* (*rray_reduce_fn)(
  r_obj* x,
  bool na_rm,
  r_ssize out_size,
  const int* v_dimensions,
  int dimensionality,
  const r_ssize* v_out_broadcast_strides,
  struct r_lazy error_call
);

typedef rray_reduce_fn (*rray_reduce_fn_switch)(
  r_obj* x,
  struct rray_arg* arg,
  struct r_lazy error_call
);

r_obj* rray_reduce(
  r_obj* x,
  r_obj* axes,
  bool na_rm,
  rray_reduce_fn_switch fn_switch,
  struct rray_arg* arg,
  struct r_lazy error_call
);

static inline r_ssize rray_reduce_count(r_ssize x_size, r_ssize out_size) {
  return out_size == 0 ? 0 : x_size / out_size;
}

#define RRAY_REDUCE(                                                           \
  X_CTYPE,                                                                     \
  X_CONST_DEREF,                                                               \
  OUT_RTYPE,                                                                   \
  OUT_CTYPE,                                                                   \
  OUT_DEREF,                                                                   \
  OUT_INIT,                                                                    \
  ONE                                                                          \
)                                                                              \
  struct rray_run_iterator it;                                                 \
  rray_run_iterator_init1(                                                     \
    &it,                                                                       \
    v_dimensions,                                                              \
    dimensionality,                                                            \
    v_out_broadcast_strides                                                    \
  );                                                                           \
                                                                               \
  r_obj* out = KEEP(r_alloc_vector(OUT_RTYPE, out_size));                      \
  OUT_CTYPE* v_out = OUT_DEREF(out);                                           \
                                                                               \
  for (r_ssize i = 0; i < out_size; ++i) {                                     \
    v_out[i] = OUT_INIT;                                                       \
  }                                                                            \
                                                                               \
  const X_CTYPE* v_x = X_CONST_DEREF(x);                                       \
                                                                               \
  for (; !rray_run_iterator_done(&it); rray_run_iterator_next1(&it)) {         \
    const r_ssize start = rray_run_iterator_start(&it);                        \
    const r_ssize end = rray_run_iterator_end(&it);                            \
                                                                               \
    r_ssize out_loc = rray_run_iterator_loc(&it, 0);                           \
    const r_ssize out_stride = rray_run_iterator_stride(&it, 0);               \
                                                                               \
    if (out_stride == 0) {                                                     \
      for (r_ssize i = start; i < end; ++i) {                                  \
        v_out[out_loc] = ONE(v_out[out_loc], v_x[i]);                          \
      }                                                                        \
    } else {                                                                   \
      for (r_ssize i = start; i < end; ++i) {                                  \
        v_out[out_loc] = ONE(v_out[out_loc], v_x[i]);                          \
        out_loc += out_stride;                                                 \
      }                                                                        \
    }                                                                          \
  }                                                                            \
                                                                               \
  FREE(1);                                                                     \
  return out;

// --------------------------------------------------------------------------
// stop_unsupported_reduce

r_no_return void stop_unsupported_reduce(
  const char* op,
  r_obj* x,
  struct rray_arg* arg,
  struct r_lazy error_call
);

#endif

#include "reduce-sum.h"

#include <stdint.h>

#include "int-128.h"
#include "reduce.h"
#include "type.h"
#include "utils.h"

#include "decl/reduce-sum-decl.h"

// Above an input length of 2^32 for a single output location, we can't
// guarantee that an `int` sum will fit in an `int64_t`. However, it is
// guaranteed to fit in an `int128_t`. So we fall back to a slower sum that
// relies on `int128_t` summing. That type isn't actually portable, so we mock
// our own minimal version.
#define RRAY_SUM_INT64_MAX_COUNT ((r_ssize) 1 << 32)

r_obj* ffi_rray_sum(
  r_obj* ffi_x,
  r_obj* ffi_axes,
  r_obj* ffi_na_rm,
  r_obj* ffi_frame
) {
  struct r_lazy error_call = {.x = ffi_frame, .env = r_null};
  const bool na_rm = r_arg_as_bool(ffi_na_rm, "na_rm");
  return rray_sum(ffi_x, ffi_axes, na_rm, rray_args.x, error_call);
}

r_obj* rray_sum(
  r_obj* x,
  r_obj* axes,
  bool na_rm,
  struct rray_arg* arg,
  struct r_lazy error_call
) {
  return rray_reduce(x, axes, na_rm, rray_sum_switch, arg, error_call);
}

static rray_reduce_fn rray_sum_switch(
  r_obj* x,
  struct rray_arg* arg,
  struct r_lazy error_call
) {
  switch (rray_typeof(x)) {
  case RRAY_TYPE_logical:
    return rray_sum_lgl;
  case RRAY_TYPE_integer:
    return rray_sum_int;
  case RRAY_TYPE_double:
    return rray_sum_dbl;
  case RRAY_TYPE_complex:
    return rray_sum_cpl;

  case RRAY_TYPE_character:
  case RRAY_TYPE_raw:
  case RRAY_TYPE_list:
    stop_unsupported_reduce("sum", x, arg, error_call);

  case RRAY_TYPE_scalar:
    stop_scalar_input(x, arg, error_call);
  }

  r_stop_unreachable();
}

static r_obj* rray_sum_lgl(
  r_obj* x,
  bool na_rm,
  r_ssize out_size,
  const int* v_dimensions,
  int dimensionality,
  const r_ssize* v_out_broadcast_strides,
  struct r_lazy error_call
) {
  const int* v_x = r_lgl_cbegin(x);

  return rray_sum_lgl_or_int(
    v_x,
    r_globals.na_lgl,
    na_rm,
    out_size,
    v_dimensions,
    dimensionality,
    v_out_broadcast_strides,
    error_call
  );
}

static r_obj* rray_sum_int(
  r_obj* x,
  bool na_rm,
  r_ssize out_size,
  const int* v_dimensions,
  int dimensionality,
  const r_ssize* v_out_broadcast_strides,
  struct r_lazy error_call
) {
  const int* v_x = r_int_cbegin(x);
  const r_ssize x_size = r_length(x);
  const r_ssize count = rray_reduce_count(x_size, out_size);

  if (count > RRAY_SUM_INT64_MAX_COUNT) {
    return rray_sum_int_fallback(
      v_x,
      na_rm,
      out_size,
      v_dimensions,
      dimensionality,
      v_out_broadcast_strides,
      error_call
    );
  }

  return rray_sum_lgl_or_int(
    v_x,
    r_globals.na_int,
    na_rm,
    out_size,
    v_dimensions,
    dimensionality,
    v_out_broadcast_strides,
    error_call
  );
}

static r_obj* rray_sum_dbl(
  r_obj* x,
  bool na_rm,
  r_ssize out_size,
  const int* v_dimensions,
  int dimensionality,
  const r_ssize* v_out_broadcast_strides,
  struct r_lazy error_call
) {
  const double* v_x = r_dbl_cbegin(x);

  r_obj* out = KEEP(r_alloc_double(out_size));
  double* v_out = r_dbl_begin(out);

  for (r_ssize i = 0; i < out_size; ++i) {
    v_out[i] = 0.0;
  }

  struct rray_run_iterator it;
  rray_run_iterator_init1(
    &it,
    v_dimensions,
    dimensionality,
    v_out_broadcast_strides
  );

  if (na_rm) {
    for (; !rray_run_iterator_done(&it); rray_run_iterator_next1(&it)) {
      const r_ssize start = rray_run_iterator_start(&it);
      const r_ssize end = rray_run_iterator_end(&it);

      r_ssize out_loc = rray_run_iterator_loc(&it, 0);
      const r_ssize out_stride = rray_run_iterator_stride(&it, 0);

      if (out_stride == 0) {
        double sum = v_out[out_loc];

        for (r_ssize i = start; i < end; ++i) {
          const double x_elt = v_x[i];
          sum += ISNAN(x_elt) ? 0 : x_elt;
        }

        v_out[out_loc] = sum;
      } else {
        for (r_ssize i = start; i < end; ++i) {
          const double x_elt = v_x[i];
          v_out[out_loc] += ISNAN(x_elt) ? 0 : x_elt;
          out_loc += out_stride;
        }
      }
    }
  } else {
    for (; !rray_run_iterator_done(&it); rray_run_iterator_next1(&it)) {
      const r_ssize start = rray_run_iterator_start(&it);
      const r_ssize end = rray_run_iterator_end(&it);

      r_ssize out_loc = rray_run_iterator_loc(&it, 0);
      const r_ssize out_stride = rray_run_iterator_stride(&it, 0);

      if (out_stride == 0) {
        double sum = v_out[out_loc];

        for (r_ssize i = start; i < end; ++i) {
          sum += v_x[i];
        }

        v_out[out_loc] = sum;
      } else {
        for (r_ssize i = start; i < end; ++i) {
          v_out[out_loc] += v_x[i];
          out_loc += out_stride;
        }
      }
    }

Thinking

Confirmed: in RRAY_REDUCE, since v_out and v_x can alias, the compiler must write to v_out[out_loc] each step rather than accumulate in a register.

make_array <- function(dimensions, type) {
  n <- prod(dimensions)

  values <- switch(
    type,
    logical = sample(c(TRUE, FALSE), n, replace = TRUE),
    integer = sample(100L, n, replace = TRUE),
    double = runif(n)
  )

  array(values, dimensions)
}

set.seed(1)

results <- list()

for (operation in names(operations)) {
  fn <- operations[[operation]]$fn

  for (type in operations[[operation]]$types) {
    for (shape in names(shapes)) {
      x <- make_array(shapes[[shape]][[1L]], type)
      axes <- shapes[[shape]][[2L]]

      measurement <- bench::mark(
        fn(x, axes),
        iterations = iterations,
        check = FALSE,
        memory = FALSE,
        filter_gc = FALSE
      )

      results[[length(results) + 1L]] <- data.frame(
        operation = operation,
        type = type,
        shape = shape,
        median_ms = as.numeric(measurement$median) * 1000
      )
    }
  }
}

results <- do.call(rbind, results)

if (side * side != size) {
  stop("`RRAY_BENCH_SIZE` must be a perfect square.", call. = FALSE)
}

make_values <- function(n, type, offset) {
  values <- (seq_len(n) * (offset * 2L + 1L)) %% 2001L - 1000L

  if (type == "integer") {
    as.integer(values)
  } else {
    as.double(values) + offset / 10
  }
}

add_missing <- function(x, type, missing, offset) {
  if (missing == "none") {
    return(x)
  }

  step <- if (missing == "sparse") 101L + offset else 2L + offset
  start <- 1L + offset

  if (start > length(x)) {
    return(x)
  }

  locations <- seq.int(start, length(x), by = step)

  if (type == "integer") {
    x[locations] <- NA_integer_
  } else {
    midpoint <- length(locations) %/% 2L
    na_locations <- locations[seq_len(midpoint)]
    nan_locations <- locations[seq.int(midpoint + 1L, length(locations))]
    x[na_locations] <- NA_real_
    x[nan_locations] <- NaN
  }

  x
}

if (side * side != size) {
  stop("`RRAY_BENCH_SIZE` must be a perfect square.", call. = FALSE)
}

dimensions <- c(side, side)

cases <- expand.grid(
  operation = c("all", "any"),
  na_rm = c(FALSE, TRUE),
  missing = c("none", "sparse", "dense"),
  values = c("true", "false", "mixed"),
  axes = c("axis1", "axis2", "both"),
  stringsAsFactors = FALSE
)

make_values <- function(n, values) {
  switch(
    values,
    true = rep(TRUE, n),
    false = rep(FALSE, n),
    mixed = (seq_len(n) * 7L) %% 3L != 0L
  )
}

add_missing <- function(x, missing) {
  if (missing == "none") {
    return(x)
  }

  step <- if (missing == "sparse") 101L else 3L
  x[seq.int(1L, length(x), by = step)] <- NA
  x
}

I made the change on feature/reduce-write, but it barely helps. Most cases are unchanged and the best one is 15% faster. Nothing got slower, and the reduce and extremum tests pass (647 pass, 0 fail).

The change (src/reduce.h): in the out_stride == 0 branch of RRAY_REDUCE, read v_out[out_loc] into a local out_elt, run ONE() on the local, and write it back once after the loop. This is the same pattern rray_sum_dbl() uses. Your reasoning holds: v_out and v_x have the same element type, so the compiler can't rule out that writing to one changes the other, and it stores on every step.

Benchmark: I benchmarked all 12 reducers that use the macro: max and min (lgl, int, dbl), prod (lgl, int, dbl, cpl), all and any. Each ran with na_rm both off and on, on four shapes, at 60 iterations, alternating main and the branch for 3 rounds. The shapes were [4000, 4000] over 1, over 2 and over 1, 2, plus [200, 200, 200] over 1, 3. Ratios are branch time divided by main time, taking the fastest of the 3 rounds:

  • Prod, double, [200, 200, 200] over 1, 3: 7.13 ms → 6.05 ms (0.85). This held in all 3 rounds.

  • Max, prod (lgl, int), all, any with na_rm = FALSE on stride 0 shapes: 0.97 to 0.99. This was steady across rounds, so the gain is real but small.

  • Everything else, including all of min and max on doubles: 1.00. The "over 2" shapes don't use the stride 0 branch at all, so they shouldn't change.

Why the gain is small: each step has to wait for the previous step's result. That chain of waiting sets the speed, whether the running value sits in a register or in memory. The CPU can also pass a just-stored value straight to the next read, which hides most of the cost of the extra store.

A bigger opportunity: the stride 0 path is much slower than the other path. For example, rray_max() on integers takes 10.06 ms over axis 1 but 1.86 ms over axis 2. The other path updates many independent outputs, so the CPU can work on several at once. Keeping a few separate running values in the stride 0 loop would get the same benefit. That's a separate change, and for double prod it would change the order of the multiplications, so the results could differ in the last few bits. I'd like your call before trying it.

Nothing is committed. The main worktree is still at /tmp/rray4-main and the benchmark script is at /tmp/bench-reduce-write.R if you want to rerun it.

Bash: cat src/one-extremum.h src/one-multiply.h src/one-logical.h | head -250; sed -n 55,90p src/re… 
cat src/one-extremum.h src/one-multiply.h src/one-logical.h | head -250; sed -n 55,90p src/reduce-prod.c
#ifndef RRAY_ONE_EXTREMUM_H
#define RRAY_ONE_EXTREMUM_H

#include "rlang.h"

#include "missing.h"
#include "utils.h"

// Each of the "one" functions below is carefully tuned to maximize
// the chance of the resulting loop being vectorized by the compiler.
// Anecdotally tested on an M2 Mac, where the assembly was analyzed.
// For doubles, the double switch is less likely to vectorize but seems
// to be the most stable when reducing across different combinations of
// axes.

// --------------------------------------------------------------------------
// Elementwise

// See `rray_pmax_int_one()`
static inline int rray_pmax_lgl_one(int x, int y) {
  const int out = x < y ? y : x;
  const bool has_na =
    bool_bitwise_or(rray_lgl_is_missing(x), rray_lgl_is_missing(y));
  return has_na ? r_globals.na_lgl : out;
}

// See `rray_pmax_int_one_na_rm()`
static inline int rray_pmax_lgl_one_na_rm(int x, int y) {
  return x < y ? y : x;
}

// Bitwise `|` improves efficiency here
// - `x = 1`, `y = 1`: returns `1` (`out = 1`, `has_na = false`).
// - `x = 1`, `y = NA`: returns `NA` (`out = 1`, `has_na = true`).
// - `x = NA`, `y = 1`: returns `NA` (`out = 1`, `has_na = true`).
// - `x = NA`, `y = NA`: returns `NA` (`out = NA`, `has_na = true`).
static inline int rray_pmax_int_one(int x, int y) {
  const int out = x < y ? y : x;
  const bool has_na =
    bool_bitwise_or(rray_int_is_missing(x), rray_int_is_missing(y));
  return has_na ? r_globals.na_int : out;
}

// Integer `NA` is `INT_MIN`
// - `x = 1`, `y = 1`: returns `1` (`out = 1`).
// - `x = 1`, `y = NA`: returns `1` (`out = 1`).
// - `x = NA`, `y = 1`: returns `1` (`out = 1`).
// - `x = NA`, `y = NA`: returns `NA` (`out = NA`).
static inline int rray_pmax_int_one_na_rm(int x, int y) {
  return x < y ? y : x;
}

static inline double rray_pmax_dbl_one(double x, double y) {
  switch (rray_dbl_classify(x)) {
  case RRAY_DBL_number: {
    switch (rray_dbl_classify(y)) {
    case RRAY_DBL_number:
      return x < y ? y : x;
    case RRAY_DBL_missing:
      return y;
    case RRAY_DBL_nan:
      return y;
    }
  }
  case RRAY_DBL_missing: {
    return x;
  }
  case RRAY_DBL_nan: {
    switch (rray_dbl_classify(y)) {
    case RRAY_DBL_number:
      return x;
    case RRAY_DBL_missing:
      return y;
    case RRAY_DBL_nan:
      return x;
    }
  }
  }
  r_stop_unreachable();
}

static inline double rray_pmax_dbl_one_na_rm(double x, double y) {
  switch (rray_dbl_classify(x)) {
  case RRAY_DBL_number: {
    switch (rray_dbl_classify(y)) {
    case RRAY_DBL_number:
      return x < y ? y : x;
    case RRAY_DBL_missing:
      return x;
    case RRAY_DBL_nan:
      return x;
    }
  }
  case RRAY_DBL_missing: {
    switch (rray_dbl_classify(y)) {
    case RRAY_DBL_number:
      return y;
    case RRAY_DBL_missing:
      return x;
    case RRAY_DBL_nan:
      return x;
    }
  }
  case RRAY_DBL_nan: {
    switch (rray_dbl_classify(y)) {
    case RRAY_DBL_number:
      return y;
    case RRAY_DBL_missing:
      return y;
    case RRAY_DBL_nan:
      return x;
    }
  }
  }
  r_stop_unreachable();
}

// See `rray_pmin_int_one()`
static inline int rray_pmin_lgl_one(int x, int y) {
  return x > y ? y : x;
}

// See `rray_pmin_int_one_na_rm()`
static inline int rray_pmin_lgl_one_na_rm(int x, int y) {
  int out = x > y ? y : x;
  out = rray_lgl_is_missing(y) ? x : out;
  out = rray_lgl_is_missing(x) ? y : out;
  return out;
}

// Integer `NA` is `INT_MIN`
// - `x = 1`, `y = 1`: returns `1` (`out = 1`).
// - `x = 1`, `y = NA`: returns `NA` (`out = NA`).
// - `x = NA`, `y = 1`: returns `NA` (`out = NA`).
// - `x = NA`, `y = NA`: returns `NA` (`out = NA`).
static inline int rray_pmin_int_one(int x, int y) {
  return x > y ? y : x;
}

// A regular minimum selects `NA`, so each missing operand is replaced by the
// other operand:
// - `x = 1`, `y = 1`: returns `1` (`out = 1`, neither replacement applies).
// - `x = 1`, `y = NA`: returns `1` (`out = NA`, replace `y` with `x`).
// - `x = NA`, `y = 1`: returns `1` (`out = NA`, replace `x` with `y`).
// - `x = NA`, `y = NA`: returns `NA` (`out = NA`, both replacements are `NA`).
static inline int rray_pmin_int_one_na_rm(int x, int y) {
  int out = x > y ? y : x;
  out = rray_int_is_missing(y) ? x : out;
  out = rray_int_is_missing(x) ? y : out;
  return out;
}

static inline double rray_pmin_dbl_one(double x, double y) {
  switch (rray_dbl_classify(x)) {
  case RRAY_DBL_number: {
    switch (rray_dbl_classify(y)) {
    case RRAY_DBL_number:
      return x > y ? y : x;
    case RRAY_DBL_missing:
      return y;
    case RRAY_DBL_nan:
      return y;
    }
  }
  case RRAY_DBL_missing: {
    return x;
  }
  case RRAY_DBL_nan: {
    switch (rray_dbl_classify(y)) {
    case RRAY_DBL_number:
      return x;
    case RRAY_DBL_missing:
      return y;
    case RRAY_DBL_nan:
      return x;
    }
  }
  }
  r_stop_unreachable();
}

static inline double rray_pmin_dbl_one_na_rm(double x, double y) {
  switch (rray_dbl_classify(x)) {
  case RRAY_DBL_number: {
    switch (rray_dbl_classify(y)) {
    case RRAY_DBL_number:
      return x > y ? y : x;
    case RRAY_DBL_missing:
      return x;
    case RRAY_DBL_nan:
      return x;
    }
  }
  case RRAY_DBL_missing: {
    switch (rray_dbl_classify(y)) {
    case RRAY_DBL_number:
      return y;
    case RRAY_DBL_missing:
      return x;
    case RRAY_DBL_nan:

Here is the stride 0 branch with four running values instead of one. Every other part of the macro stays the same.

    if (out_stride == 0) {                                                     \
      OUT_CTYPE out_elt0 = v_out[out_loc];                                     \
      OUT_CTYPE out_elt1 = OUT_INIT;                                           \
      OUT_CTYPE out_elt2 = OUT_INIT;                                           \
      OUT_CTYPE out_elt3 = OUT_INIT;                                           \
                                                                               \
      r_ssize i = start;                                                       \
                                                                               \
      for (; i + 4 <= end; i += 4) {                                           \
        out_elt0 = ONE(out_elt0, v_x[i]);                                      \
        out_elt1 = ONE(out_elt1, v_x[i + 1]);                                  \
        out_elt2 = ONE(out_elt2, v_x[i + 2]);                                  \
        out_elt3 = ONE(out_elt3, v_x[i + 3]);                                  \
      }                                                                        \
                                                                               \
      for (; i < end; ++i) {                                                   \
        out_elt0 = ONE(out_elt0, v_x[i]);                                      \
      }                                                                        \
                                                                               \
      out_elt0 = COMBINE(out_elt0, out_elt1);                                  \
      out_elt2 = COMBINE(out_elt2, out_elt3);                                  \
      v_out[out_loc] = COMBINE(out_elt0, out_elt2);                            \
    } else {                                                                   \

The four updates in each step don't depend on each other, so the CPU can work on them at the same time. Each one still waits on its own previous value, but now four chains run side by side instead of one.

The catch is COMBINE, a new macro parameter. ONE(out, x) takes an output value and an input value. Merging two running values takes two output values, and for some reducers that is a different function:

  • max, min, all, any: the input and output types match, so the existing elementwise helpers work as COMBINE, for example rray_pmax_int_one(). For double max without na_rm, rray_pmax_dbl_one() gives the same answer in any order: a missing value beats a number, and NA beats NaN. So merging in a different order is safe.

  • prod: logical and integer inputs produce a double output, so ONE is (double, int) and can't merge two doubles. COMBINE would be a plain double multiply, plus a complex multiply for complex.

Where results can change: only for prod on doubles and complex values. The multiplications run in a different order, so:

  • Results can differ in the last few bits.

  • Overflow can depend on the order. For example, 1e300 * 1e300 * 1e-300 gives Inf from left to right, but 1e300 if the last two are multiplied first.

  • When both NA and NaN appear, which one comes out can change. Base R already doesn't promise which one you get there.

Max, min, all and any would give exactly the same results as now.

Two things I'd check before trusting it:

  • Whether clang already turns the integer max/min loops into SIMD code. That would explain why they are slow only on the stride 0 path. If it does, four running values could make things worse. I'd look at the assembly for rray_max_int() first.

  • The double extremum helpers branch on what kind of value each one is. The comment in one-extremum.h says they were tuned by hand for vectorization. Running four chains through a branchy function may not speed up much, so they need their own benchmark.

I haven't made this change. If you want it, I'd do it as a separate change on top of feature/reduce-write and run the same benchmark.

Davis

Look at bullet 1

	sub	x16, x16, #1
	ldr	x17, [sp, #1064]

LBB10_84:                               ;   Parent Loop BB10_76 Depth=1
	ldr	w17, [x15], #4
	ldr	w1, [x16]
	cmp	w1, w17
	csel	w17, w1, w17, lt
	str	w17, [x16]
	add	x16, x16, x14
	subs	x13, x13, #1
	b.ne	LBB10_84
	b	LBB10_94

	ldp	q6, q7, [x14], #64
	smin.4s	v0, v0, v4
	smin.4s	v1, v1, v5
	smin.4s	v2, v2, v6
	smin.4s	v3, v3, v7

	b.ne	LBB10_89
	smin.4s	v0, v0, v1
	smin.4s	v0, v0, v2

	csel	w14, w14, w16, lt

Your hunch was right: clang already turns some of these stride 0 loops into SIMD code, so four running values would do nothing for them. It does this whenever ONE() is a plain max or min. The slow cases are the ones where the NA handling hides the max or min from clang. For those, the fix is to make the max or min visible again, not to add more running values.

I compiled src/reduce-extremum.c at -O2 (arm64) and read the stride 0 loops in rray_max_int() and rray_min_int().

Already SIMD (about 1 ms on 16M elements):

  • rray_max_int() with na_rm = TRUE: rray_max_int_one_na_rm() is a plain max, since integer NA is INT_MIN. clang emits four smax.4s running values of 4 lanes each, so 16 elements at once, and combines them at the end. That is exactly my proposal, already done by the compiler.

  • rray_min_int() with na_rm = FALSE: same thing with smin.4s, because a plain min already returns NA when one is present.

Scalar, one element at a time (about 9 to 10 ms):

  • rray_max_int() with na_rm = FALSE:

    ldr   w16, [x12], #4
cmp   w14, w16
csel  w17, w14, w16, gt     ; max
cmp   w15, w16
ccmp  w15, w14, #4, ne      ; either one NA?
csel  w14, w15, w17, eq     ; then NA

    has_na ? NA : out doesn't look like a max to clang anymore, so it falls back to a chain of steps that each wait on the last.

  • rray_min_int() with na_rm = TRUE: same problem. The "replace NA with the other value" selects break the min pattern.

This matches the benchmark exactly: max is about 10x slower without na_rm, and min is about 10x slower with it.

What I'd do instead: split each slow loop into two reductions that clang knows how to vectorize. For max without na_rm:

int out_elt = v_out[out_loc];
bool any_na = out_elt == r_globals.na_int;

for (r_ssize i = start; i < end; ++i) {
  const int x_elt = v_x[i];
  out_elt = out_elt < x_elt ? x_elt : out_elt;
  any_na |= x_elt == r_globals.na_int;
}

v_out[out_loc] = any_na ? r_globals.na_int : out_elt;

any_na has to start from the current output value. Otherwise an NA saved by an earlier run would be lost, because max(NA, x) gives x. For min with na_rm, swap each NA for INT_MAX before the min. That is a select followed by a min, which clang also vectorizes. Both versions give the same results in any order, so nothing changes for users. They should land near the ~1 ms of the fast cases.

What this means for the macro: neither shape fits ONE(out, x). Those two cases (and the logical versions) would need their own stride 0 loop, or a second macro built around the max-plus-flag shape.

The other slow cases need a separate look:

  • all and any are about 14 ms either way, so both modes are scalar.

  • The double extremum helpers branch on what kind of value each one is, which clang can't vectorize.

  • Prod on doubles is a true multiply chain. clang won't reorder it because that changes rounding, so there four running values is the only option, with the rounding caveat from before.

I haven't changed any code. feature/reduce-write still only has the local variable change from before. Should I try the split loops for int and lgl max and min there next, or look at the all and any assembly first?

Davis

r_obj* ffi_rray_max(
  r_obj* ffi_x,
  r_obj* ffi_axes,
  r_obj* ffi_na_rm,
  r_obj* ffi_frame
) {
  struct r_lazy error_call = {.x = ffi_frame, .env = r_null};
  const bool na_rm = r_arg_as_bool(ffi_na_rm, "na_rm");
  return rray_max(ffi_x, ffi_axes, na_rm, rray_args.x, error_call);
}

r_obj* ffi_rray_min(
  r_obj* ffi_x,
  r_obj* ffi_axes,
  r_obj* ffi_na_rm,
  r_obj* ffi_frame
) {
  struct r_lazy error_call = {.x = ffi_frame, .env = r_null};
static r_obj* rray_max_int(
  r_obj* x,
  bool na_rm,
  r_ssize out_size,
  const int* v_dimensions,
  int dimensionality,
  const r_ssize* v_out_broadcast_strides,
  struct r_lazy error_call
) {
  if (na_rm) {
    RRAY_REDUCE(
      int,
      r_int_cbegin,
      R_TYPE_integer,
      int,
      r_int_begin,
      -INT_MAX,
      rray_max_int_one_na_rm
    );
  } else {
    RRAY_REDUCE(
      int,
      r_int_cbegin,
      R_TYPE_integer,
      int,
      r_int_begin,
      -INT_MAX,
      rray_max_int_one
    );
  }
}

static r_obj* rray_max_dbl(
  r_obj* x,
  bool na_rm,
  r_ssize out_size,
  const int* v_dimensions,
  int dimensionality,
  const r_ssize* v_out_broadcast_strides,
  struct r_lazy error_call
) {
  if (na_rm) {
    RRAY_REDUCE(
      double,
      r_dbl_cbegin,
      R_TYPE_double,
      double,
      r_dbl_begin,
      -INFINITY,

      double,
      r_dbl_cbegin,
      R_TYPE_double,
      double,
      r_dbl_begin,
      -INFINITY,
      rray_max_dbl_one
    );
  }
}

static inline int rray_max_lgl_one_na_rm(int out, int x) {
  return rray_pmax_lgl_one_na_rm(out, x);
}

static inline int rray_max_int_one(int out, int x) {
  return rray_pmax_int_one(out, x);
}

static inline int rray_max_int_one_na_rm(int out, int x) {
  return rray_pmax_int_one_na_rm(out, x);
}

static inline double rray_max_dbl_one(double out, double x) {
  return rray_pmax_dbl_one(out, x);
}

// Comparisons with `NA` or `NaN` are false, and `out` is never missing
// - `out = 1`, `x = 2`: returns `2`.
// - `out = 2`, `x = 1`: returns `2`.
// - `out = 1`, `x = NA`: returns `1`.
// - `out = 1`, `x = NaN`: returns `1`.
static inline double rray_max_dbl_one_na_rm(double out, double x) {
  return out < x ? x : out;
}

static inline int rray_min_lgl_one(int out, int x) {
  return rray_pmin_lgl_one(out, x);
}

// Logical `NA` is `INT_MIN`, and `out` is never `NA`
// - `out = 1`, `x = 0`: returns `0` (`is_less = true`).
// - `out = 0`, `x = 1`: returns `0` (`is_less = false`).
// - `out = 1`, `x = NA`: returns `1` (`is_less = false`).
static inline int rray_min_lgl_one_na_rm(int out, int x) {
  const bool is_less = bool_bitwise_and(x < out, !rray_lgl_is_missing(x));
  return is_less ? x : out;
}

static inline int rray_min_int_one(int out, int x) {
  return rray_pmin_int_one(out, x);
}

// Integer `NA` is `INT_MIN`, and `out` is never `NA`
// - `out = 2`, `x = 1`: returns `1` (`is_less = true`).
// - `out = 1`, `x = 2`: returns `1` (`is_less = false`).
// - `out = 1`, `x = NA`: returns `1` (`is_less = false`).
static inline int rray_min_int_one_na_rm(int out, int x) {
  const bool is_less = bool_bitwise_and(x < out, !rray_int_is_missing(x));
  return is_less ? x : out;
}

static inline double rray_min_dbl_one(double out, double x) {
  return rray_pmin_dbl_one(out, x);
}

// Comparisons with `NA` or `NaN` are false, and `out` is never missing
// - `out = 2`, `x = 1`: returns `1`.
// - `out = 1`, `x = 2`: returns `1`.
// - `out = 1`, `x = NA`: returns `1`.
// - `out = 1`, `x = NaN`: returns `1`.
static inline double rray_min_dbl_one_na_rm(double out, double x) {
  return out > x ? x : out;
}

#ifndef RRAY_MISSING_H

#include "utils.h"

static inline bool rray_lgl_is_missing(int x) {
  return x == r_globals.na_lgl;
}

static inline bool rray_int_is_missing(int x) {
  return x == r_globals.na_int;
}

static inline bool rray_dbl_is_missing(double x) {
  return ISNAN(x);
}

static inline bool rray_cpl_is_missing(r_complex x) {
  // Purposefully bitwise, can help compiler perform vectorization
  return bool_bitwise_or(ISNAN(x.r), ISNAN(x.i));
}

iterations <- 60L
output <- Sys.getenv("RRAY_BENCH_OUTPUT")
ops <- list(max = rray_max, min = rray_min)
shapes <- list(
  "[4000, 4000] over 1" = list(c(4000L, 4000L), 1L),
  "[4000, 4000] over 2" = list(c(4000L, 4000L), 2L),
  "[4000, 4000] over 1, 2" = list(c(4000L, 4000L), 1:2),
  "[200, 200, 200] over 1, 3" = list(c(200L, 200L, 200L), c(1L, 3L)),
  "[10, 1e6] over 1" = list(c(10L, 1000000L), 1L)
)
set.seed(1)
res <- list()
for (missing in c("none", "sparse")) for (s in names(shapes)) {
  d <- shapes[[s]][[1]]
  x <- array(sample(100L, prod(d), TRUE), d)
  if (missing == "sparse") x[seq(1, length(x), by = 101)] <- NA
  for (op in names(ops)) for (na_rm in c(FALSE, TRUE)) {
    fn <- ops[[op]]
    m <- bench::mark(fn(x, shapes[[s]][[2]], na_rm = na_rm), iterations = iterations, check = FALSE, memory = FALSE, filter_gc = FALSE)
    res[[length(res) + 1]] <- data.frame(op = op, na_rm = na_rm, missing = missing, shape = s, ms = as.numeric(m$median) * 1000)
  }

Thinking

Stride 0 cases show 5-10x speedups, but min with na_rm over axis 2 is consistently 26% slower due to the rewritten rray_min_int_one_na_rm() helper performing worse in the non-stride-0 loop. I'll restore the old helper and give min its own stride 0 loop like max, then rerun the benchmarks.

+  r_obj* out = KEEP(r_alloc_integer(out_size));

+    const r_ssize start = rray_run_iterator_start(&it);
+    const r_ssize end = rray_run_iterator_end(&it);
+
+    r_ssize out_loc = rray_run_iterator_loc(&it, 0);
+    const r_ssize out_stride = rray_run_iterator_stride(&it, 0);
+
+    if (out_stride == 0) {
+      int out_elt = v_out[out_loc];
+
+      for (r_ssize i = start; i < end; ++i) {
+        int x_elt = v_x[i];
+        x_elt = rray_int_is_missing(x_elt) ? INT_MAX : x_elt;
+        out_elt = x_elt < out_elt ? x_elt : out_elt;
+      }
+
+      v_out[out_loc] = out_elt;
+    } else {
+      for (r_ssize i = start; i < end; ++i) {
+        v_out[out_loc] = rray_min_int_one_na_rm(v_out[out_loc], v_x[i]);
+        out_loc += out_stride;
+      }
+    }
+  }
+
+  FREE(1);
+  return out;
+}

Yes, it's much faster. On every shape where the output has stride 0, int max without na_rm and int min with na_rm are now 5 to 10x faster than main. No case got slower.

Results (ms, fastest of 3 rounds, data with sparse NA):

Case Shape main branch ratio
max, na_rm = FALSE [4000, 4000] over 1 10.06 1.03 0.10
max, na_rm = FALSE [4000, 4000] over 1, 2 9.97 1.02 0.10
max, na_rm = FALSE [200, 200, 200] over 1, 3 5.07 0.58 0.11
max, na_rm = FALSE [10, 1e6] over 1 6.29 3.55 0.56
min, na_rm = TRUE [4000, 4000] over 1 9.03 1.45 0.16
min, na_rm = TRUE [4000, 4000] over 1, 2 9.01 1.43 0.16
min, na_rm = TRUE [200, 200, 200] over 1, 3 4.51 0.84 0.19
min, na_rm = TRUE [10, 1e6] over 1 4.99 4.98 1.00

  • Everything else (the other two max/min cases and every "over 2" shape): between 0.94 and 1.01.

  • Data with and without NA: about the same speed, because the new loops don't branch.

  • [10, 1e6] over 1: the gain is smaller here because each run is only 10 elements, too short to fill the SIMD loop. For min with na_rm there is no gain at all on this shape.

The change (src/reduce-extremum.c): rray_max_int() and rray_min_int() still use RRAY_REDUCE for the cases that were already fast. The slow case in each now has a hand-written loop, in the same style as rray_sum_dbl(). Only the stride 0 branch is different:

  • max without na_rm: a plain max, plus a separate any_missing flag. any_missing starts from the current output value, so an NA saved by an earlier run is kept.

  • min with na_rm: each NA is swapped for INT_MAX before a plain min.

The other branch still calls the existing rray_max_int_one() / rray_min_int_one_na_rm() helpers.

A first attempt that I backed out: I first rewrote rray_min_int_one_na_rm() itself, so the macro could stay unchanged. That made the stride 0 loop fast, but min with na_rm over axis 2 got 26% slower (1.25 → 1.58 ms) in all 3 rounds. The new helper did worse in the other loop. The hand-written loop avoids that.

Checks:

  • The reduce and extremum tests pass (647 pass, 0 fail).

  • I compared against base R's max() and min() through apply() on 300 random 3D arrays. They mixed in NA, INT_MAX and -INT_MAX, covering every subset of axes and both na_rm settings, 4,200 results in all. Every result matched.

  • The assembly shows smax.4s (with cmeq/orr for the missing flag) and smin.4s in the new stride 0 loops.

  • No new r_obj* values need protecting: out is wrapped in KEEP() and freed right before it is returned.

Downsides:

  • Both functions now repeat the body of RRAY_REDUCE, about 40 lines each.

  • Min with na_rm (about 1.45 ms) is still slower than the fast cases (about 1.0 ms), because of the extra swap on every element.

  • The logical versions have the same two slow cases (rray_max_lgl() without na_rm and rray_min_lgl() with it). The same fix should work there, so with all four we'd want a shared macro rather than four copies.

Nothing is committed. The branch has the RRAY_REDUCE local variable change plus this prototype.

Davis

// -----------------------------------------------------------------------------

#ifdef WORDS_BIGENDIAN
static const int rray_dbl_indicator_pos = 1;
#else
static const int rray_dbl_indicator_pos = 0;
#endif

union rray_dbl_indicator {
  double value;
  unsigned int key[2];
};

enum rray_dbl_class {
  RRAY_DBL_number,
  RRAY_DBL_missing,
  RRAY_DBL_nan
};

static inline enum rray_dbl_class rray_dbl_classify(double x) {
  if (!isnan(x)) {
    return RRAY_DBL_number;
  }

  union rray_dbl_indicator indicator;
  indicator.value = x;

  if (indicator.key[rray_dbl_indicator_pos] == 1954) {
    return RRAY_DBL_missing;
  } else {
    return RRAY_DBL_nan;
  }
}

#endif

Thinking

The assembly shows clang keeps these double loops scalar (no auto-vectorization without -ffast-math), so I'll hand-vectorize with four running accumulators, tracking a NaN flag when na_rm is off and falling back to the exact scalar routine for any run containing NaN to preserve bit-identical results—prototyping this for max first.

+  struct rray_run_iterator it;
+  rray_run_iterator_init1(
+    &it,
+    v_dimensions,
+    dimensionality,
+    v_out_broadcast_strides
+  );
+
+  r_obj* out = KEEP(r_alloc_double(out_size));
+  double* v_out = r_dbl_begin(out);
+
+  for (r_ssize i = 0; i < out_size; ++i) {
+    v_out[i] = -INFINITY;
+  }
+
+  const double* v_x = r_dbl_cbegin(x);
+
+  for (; !rray_run_iterator_done(&it); rray_run_iterator_next1(&it)) {
+    const r_ssize start = rray_run_iterator_start(&it);
+    const r_ssize end = rray_run_iterator_end(&it);
+
+    r_ssize out_loc = rray_run_iterator_loc(&it, 0);
+    const r_ssize out_stride = rray_run_iterator_stride(&it, 0);
+
+    if (out_stride == 0) {
+      const double out_elt = v_out[out_loc];
+
+      if (na_rm) {
+        v_out[out_loc] = rray_max_dbl_run_na_rm(v_x, start, end, out_elt);
+      } else {
+        v_out[out_loc] = rray_max_dbl_run(v_x, start, end, out_elt);
+      }
+    } else if (na_rm) {
+      for (r_ssize i = start; i < end; ++i) {
+        v_out[out_loc] = rray_max_dbl_one_na_rm(v_out[out_loc], v_x[i]);
+        out_loc += out_stride;
+      }
+    } else {
+      for (r_ssize i = start; i < end; ++i) {
+        v_out[out_loc] = rray_max_dbl_one(v_out[out_loc], v_x[i]);
+        out_loc += out_stride;
+      }
+    }
+  }
+
+  FREE(1);
+  return out;
+}
+
+static inline double rray_max_dbl_run_na_rm(
+  const double* v_x,
+  r_ssize start,
+  r_ssize end,
+  double out
+) {
+  double out0 = out;
+  double out1 = -INFINITY;
+  double out2 = -INFINITY;
+  double out3 = -INFINITY;
+
+  r_ssize i = start;
+
+  for (; i + 4 <= end; i += 4) {
+    out0 = rray_max_dbl_one_na_rm(out0, v_x[i]);
+    out1 = rray_max_dbl_one_na_rm(out1, v_x[i + 1]);
+    out2 = rray_max_dbl_one_na_rm(out2, v_x[i + 2]);
+    out3 = rray_max_dbl_one_na_rm(out3, v_x[i + 3]);
+  }
+
+  for (; i < end; ++i) {
+    out0 = rray_max_dbl_one_na_rm(out0, v_x[i]);
+  }
+
+  out0 = rray_max_dbl_one_na_rm(out0, out1);
+  out2 = rray_max_dbl_one_na_rm(out2, out3);
+  return rray_max_dbl_one_na_rm(out0, out2);
+}
+
+static inline double rray_max_dbl_run(
+  const double* v_x,
+  r_ssize start,
+  r_ssize end,
+  double out
+) {
+  if (isnan(out)) {
+    return rray_max_dbl_run_slow(v_x, start, end, out);
+  }
+
+  double out0 = out;
+  double out1 = -INFINITY;
+  double out2 = -INFINITY;
+  double out3 = -INFINITY;
+
+  bool any_nan = false;
+
+  r_ssize i = start;
+
+  for (; i + 4 <= end; i += 4) {
+    const double x0 = v_x[i];
+    const double x1 = v_x[i + 1];
+    const double x2 = v_x[i + 2];
+    const double x3 = v_x[i + 3];
+
+    out0 = rray_max_dbl_one_na_rm(out0, x0);
+    out1 = rray_max_dbl_one_na_rm(out1, x1);
+    out2 = rray_max_dbl_one_na_rm(out2, x2);
+    out3 = rray_max_dbl_one_na_rm(out3, x3);
+
+    any_nan = bool_bitwise_or(
+      any_nan,
+      bool_bitwise_or(
+        bool_bitwise_or(isnan(x0), isnan(x1)),
+        bool_bitwise_or(isnan(x2), isnan(x3))
+      )
+    );
+  }
+
+  for (; i < end; ++i) {
+    const double x_elt = v_x[i];
+    out0 = rray_max_dbl_one_na_rm(out0, x_elt);
+    any_nan = bool_bitwise_or(any_nan, isnan(x_elt));
+  }
+
+  if (any_nan) {
+    return rray_max_dbl_run_slow(v_x, start, end, out);
+  }
+
+  out0 = rray_max_dbl_one_na_rm(out0, out1);
+  out2 = rray_max_dbl_one_na_rm(out2, out3);
+  return rray_max_dbl_one_na_rm(out0, out2);
+}
+
+static double rray_max_dbl_run_slow(
+  const double* v_x,
+  r_ssize start,
+  r_ssize end,
+  double out
+) {
+  for (r_ssize i = start; i < end; ++i) {
+    out = rray_max_dbl_one(out, v_x[i]);
+  }
+  return out;
+}

Write /tmp/fmax-test.c 
#include <math.h>
#include <stdbool.h>

double select_max(const double* v_x, long n, double out) {
  for (long i = 0; i < n; ++i) {
    const double x = v_x[i];
    out = out < x ? x : out;
  }
  return out;
}

double fmax_max(const double* v_x, long n, double out) {
  for (long i = 0; i < n; ++i) {
    out = fmax(out, v_x[i]);
  }
  return out;
}

double fmax_max_flag(const double* v_x, long n, double out, bool* p_any_nan) {
  int any_nan = 0;
  for (long i = 0; i < n; ++i) {
    const double x = v_x[i];
    out = fmax(out, x);
    any_nan |= x != x;
  }
  *p_any_nan = any_nan;
  return out;
}

Write /tmp/key-test.c 
#include <math.h>
#include <stdbool.h>
#include <stdint.h>
#include <string.h>

static inline int64_t dbl_key(double x) {
  int64_t bits;
  memcpy(&bits, &x, sizeof(bits));
  return bits ^ ((bits >> 63) & INT64_MAX);
}

static inline double key_dbl(int64_t key) {
  int64_t bits = key ^ ((key >> 63) & INT64_MAX);
  double x;
  memcpy(&x, &bits, sizeof(x));
  return x;
}

static inline bool key_is_nan(int64_t key) {
  int64_t magnitude = key ^ ((key >> 63) & INT64_MAX);
  return (magnitude & INT64_MAX) > 0x7ff0000000000000;
}

double key_max_na_rm(const double* v_x, long n, double out) {
  int64_t out_key = dbl_key(out);
  for (long i = 0; i < n; ++i) {
    int64_t key = dbl_key(v_x[i]);
    key = key_is_nan(key) ? INT64_MIN : key;
    out_key = out_key < key ? key : out_key;
  }
  return key_dbl(out_key);
}

double key_max_flag(const double* v_x, long n, double out, bool* p_any_nan) {
  int64_t out_key = dbl_key(out);
  int any_nan = 0;
  for (long i = 0; i < n; ++i) {
    int64_t key = dbl_key(v_x[i]);
    const bool is_nan = key_is_nan(key);
    key = is_nan ? INT64_MIN : key;
    out_key = out_key < key ? key : out_key;
    any_nan |= is_nan;
  }
  *p_any_nan = any_nan;
  return key_dbl(out_key);
}

Thinking

The integer-bit-flip trick works for comparing doubles too, and clang vectorizes it without needing -ffast-math—unlike plain fmax(), which stays scalar. I'll swap this approach in for my 4-accumulator edit now.

+static inline int64_t rray_dbl_bits(double x) {
+  int64_t out;
+  memcpy(&out, &x, sizeof(out));
+  return out;
+}
+
+static inline double rray_bits_dbl(int64_t x) {
+  double out;
+  memcpy(&out, &x, sizeof(out));
+  return out;
+}
+
+static inline bool rray_bits_is_nan(int64_t x) {
+  return (x & INT64_MAX) > 0x7ff0000000000000;
+}
+
+static inline int64_t rray_dbl_key(int64_t x) {
+  return x ^ ((x >> 63) & INT64_MAX);
+}

iterations <- 40L

  "[4000, 4000] over 1" = list(c(4000L, 4000L), 1L),

)

res <- list()
for (missing in c("none", "sparse")) for (s in names(shapes)) {
  d <- shapes[[s]][[1]]
  x <- array(runif(prod(d)), d)
  if (missing == "sparse") x[seq(1, length(x), by = 100003)] <- NA

    m <- bench::mark(rray_max(x, shapes[[s]][[2]], na_rm = na_rm), iterations = iterations, check = FALSE, memory = FALSE, filter_gc = FALSE)

Thinking

I'm seeing big speedups (4-5x) on most long-stride runs, but one regression: a run with an early NA forces the whole pass onto the slow path. I'll fix this by processing in 1024-element blocks, so only blocks containing a NaN fall back to the slow path and we can short-circuit once the running value becomes NA.

Edit src/reduce-extremum.c 
-  if (isnan(out)) {
-    return rray_max_dbl_run_slow(v_x, start, end, out);
-  }
-
-  int64_t out_key = rray_dbl_key(rray_dbl_bits(out));
-  bool any_nan = false;
-
-  for (r_ssize i = start; i < end; ++i) {
-    const int64_t bits = rray_dbl_bits(v_x[i]);
-    const bool is_nan = rray_bits_is_nan(bits);
-    const int64_t key = is_nan ? INT64_MIN : rray_dbl_key(bits);
-    out_key = out_key < key ? key : out_key;
-    any_nan = bool_bitwise_or(any_nan, is_nan);
-  }
-
-  if (any_nan) {
-    return rray_max_dbl_run_slow(v_x, start, end, out);
-  }
-
-  return rray_bits_dbl(rray_dbl_key(out_key));
-}
+  const int64_t init_key = rray_dbl_key(rray_dbl_bits(-INFINITY));
+
+  while (start < end) {
+    if (rray_dbl_classify(out) == RRAY_DBL_missing) {
+      return out;
+    }
+
+    const r_ssize block_end = end - start > 1024 ? start + 1024 : end;
+
+    int64_t block_key = init_key;
+    bool any_nan = false;
+
+    for (r_ssize i = start; i < block_end; ++i) {
+      const int64_t bits = rray_dbl_bits(v_x[i]);
+      const bool is_nan = rray_bits_is_nan(bits);
+      const int64_t key = is_nan ? INT64_MIN : rray_dbl_key(bits);
+      block_key = block_key < key ? key : block_key;
+      any_nan = bool_bitwise_or(any_nan, is_nan);
+    }
+
+    if (any_nan) {
+      out = rray_max_dbl_run_slow(v_x, start, block_end, out);
+    } else {
+      out = rray_max_dbl_one(out, rray_bits_dbl(rray_dbl_key(block_key)));
+    }
+
+    start = block_end;
+  }
+
+  return out;
+}

Davis

Too complicated

Claude

Agreed. The non-na_rm version has too many parts: blocks, a slow fallback, and an early exit. I've stopped and haven't changed anything else.

The branch still has all of the double max prototype as it was at the last benchmark. That is the na_rm key loop, the blocked non-na_rm loop, and four bit helpers in src/reduce-extremum.c, plus their declarations.

The simple part is the na_rm = TRUE case on its own. It's one short loop with no fallback:

int64_t out_key = rray_dbl_key(rray_dbl_bits(out));

for (r_ssize i = start; i < end; ++i) {
  const int64_t bits = rray_dbl_bits(v_x[i]);
  const int64_t key = rray_bits_is_nan(bits) ? INT64_MIN : rray_dbl_key(bits);
  out_key = out_key < key ? key : out_key;
}

return rray_bits_dbl(rray_dbl_key(out_key));

That alone gave 3 to 4x on long stride 0 runs (17.9 → 4.6 ms on [4000, 4000] over 1). It also gives the same results as main, apart from one change: a max over a mix of 0 and -0 now always returns 0, where main and base R return whichever came first.

The na_rm = FALSE case is where all the extra parts came from, because NA has to win over NaN. There's no simple way to keep that rule and stay fast.

Which do you want?

  1. Keep only the na_rm = TRUE key loop for double max and min, and put na_rm = FALSE back on RRAY_REDUCE.

  2. Drop the double work entirely, and keep only the int max/min change and the RRAY_REDUCE local variable change.

Davis

Is it possible to design a function that uses v_missing to track if any are missing for that out location, but otherwise the main loop “ignores” missing values ? And after the main loop we have a second loop that fixes up any missings? I think like sum?

Claude

  const int* v_dimensions,
  int dimensionality,
  const r_ssize* v_out_broadcast_strides,
  struct r_lazy error_call
) {

  // Initializes with 0
  r_obj* sums = KEEP_N(r_alloc_raw0(out_size * sizeof(int64_t)), &n_prot);
  int64_t* v_sums = (int64_t*) r_raw_begin(sums);

  bool* v_missings = NULL;

  if (!na_rm) {
    // Initializes with `false`
    r_obj* missings = KEEP_N(r_alloc_raw0(out_size * sizeof(bool)), &n_prot);
    v_missings = (bool*) r_raw_begin(missings);
  }

  struct rray_run_iterator it;
  rray_run_iterator_init1(
    &it,
    v_dimensions,
    dimensionality,
    v_out_broadcast_strides
  );

  if (na_rm) {
    for (; !rray_run_iterator_done(&it); rray_run_iterator_next1(&it)) {
      const r_ssize start = rray_run_iterator_start(&it);
      const r_ssize end = rray_run_iterator_end(&it);

      r_ssize out_loc = rray_run_iterator_loc(&it, 0);
      const r_ssize out_stride = rray_run_iterator_stride(&it, 0);

      if (out_stride == 0) {
        int64_t sum = v_sums[out_loc];

        for (r_ssize i = start; i < end; ++i) {
          const int x_elt = v_x[i];
          sum += x_elt == na_value ? 0 : x_elt;
        }

        v_sums[out_loc] = sum;
      } else {
        for (r_ssize i = start; i < end; ++i) {
          const int x_elt = v_x[i];
          v_sums[out_loc] += x_elt == na_value ? 0 : x_elt;
          out_loc += out_stride;
        }
      }
    }
  } else {
    for (; !rray_run_iterator_done(&it); rray_run_iterator_next1(&it)) {
      const r_ssize start = rray_run_iterator_start(&it);
      const r_ssize end = rray_run_iterator_end(&it);

      r_ssize out_loc = rray_run_iterator_loc(&it, 0);
      const r_ssize out_stride = rray_run_iterator_stride(&it, 0);

      if (out_stride == 0) {
        int64_t sum = v_sums[out_loc];
        bool missing = v_missings[out_loc];

        for (r_ssize i = start; i < end; ++i) {
          const int x_elt = v_x[i];
          const bool na = x_elt == na_value;
          sum += na ? 0 : x_elt;
          missing |= na;
        }

        v_sums[out_loc] = sum;
        v_missings[out_loc] = missing;
      } else {
        for (r_ssize i = start; i < end; ++i) {
          const int x_elt = v_x[i];
          const bool na = x_elt == na_value;
          v_sums[out_loc] += na ? 0 : x_elt;
          v_missings[out_loc] |= na;
          out_loc += out_stride;
        }
      }
    }
  }

  r_obj* out = KEEP_N(r_alloc_integer(out_size), &n_prot);
  int* v_out = r_int_begin(out);

  if (na_rm) {
    for (r_ssize i = 0; i < out_size; ++i) {
      const int64_t sum = v_sums[i];

      if (sum > INT_MAX || sum < -INT_MAX) {
        stop_int_overflow(error_call);
      }

      v_out[i] = (int) sum;
    }
  } else {
    for (r_ssize i = 0; i < out_size; ++i) {
      if (v_missings[i]) {
        v_out[i] = r_globals.na_int;
        continue;
      }

      const int64_t sum = v_sums[i];

      if (sum > INT_MAX || sum < -INT_MAX) {
        stop_int_overflow(error_call);
      }

Yes, and it removes all three extra parts: the blocks, the slow fallback and the early exit. It's the v_missings pattern from rray_sum_int(), with one change. Doubles have two kinds of missing value, and NA beats NaN, so a bool per output isn't enough. Use a small number per output instead (0 = none, 1 = NaN, 2 = NA) and combine them with max.

Main loop. Missing values are skipped, and the worst missing kind seen so far is tracked. In the stride 0 case:

int64_t out_key = v_keys[out_loc];
uint8_t missing = v_missings[out_loc];

for (r_ssize i = start; i < end; ++i) {
  const int64_t bits = rray_dbl_bits(v_x[i]);
  const bool is_nan = rray_bits_is_nan(bits);
  const bool is_na = bool_bitwise_and(is_nan, (uint32_t) bits == 1954);
  const int64_t key = is_nan ? INT64_MIN : rray_dbl_key(bits);
  out_key = out_key < key ? key : out_key;
  missing = missing < is_nan + is_na ? is_nan + is_na : missing;
}

v_keys[out_loc] = out_key;
v_missings[out_loc] = missing;

The other branch is the same, writing v_keys[out_loc] and v_missings[out_loc] directly.

Fix-up loop:

for (r_ssize i = 0; i < out_size; ++i) {
  switch (v_missings[i]) {
  case 0: v_out[i] = rray_bits_dbl(rray_dbl_key(v_keys[i])); break;
  case 1: v_out[i] = R_NaN; break;
  case 2: v_out[i] = r_globals.na_dbl; break;
  }
}

With na_rm = TRUE you just skip v_missings and the switch, like sum does. Both modes share the main loop.

What this gets us:

  • The stride 0 loop is all integer work, so clang can turn it into SIMD code, like it did for the int prototype.

  • The other branch loses its branchy classify step, so na_rm = FALSE over axis 2 (13.6 ms today) should speed up too. My blocked version left that case untouched.

  • The speed doesn't depend on how many missing values there are or where they are.

What we give up:

  • It needs one extra byte buffer of size out_size, but only when na_rm = FALSE, same as sum.

  • A NaN result is always the standard NaN, not the exact NaN bits from the input. R can't tell them apart (identical() only separates NA from NaN).

  • A max over 0 and -0 returns 0, as before.

The part that's still clever is the int64 key: flip the bits of negative doubles so their order matches as integers. Comparing doubles directly with out < x ? x : out would skip NaN for free, but then clang won't vectorize the stride 0 loop without -ffast-math. The key costs two helpers of one line each. We could skip it if we're OK with the stride 0 case staying scalar.

The int max/min prototype could use the same bool v_missings shape as sum, in place of my local any_missing flag, so all of these would follow one pattern.

Should I swap the double max prototype for this and benchmark it?

Davis

267	static inline double rray_max_dbl_run_na_rm(
268	  const double* v_x,
269	  r_ssize start,
270	  r_ssize end,
271	  double out
272	) {
273	  int64_t out_key = rray_dbl_key(rray_dbl_bits(out));
274	
275	  for (r_ssize i = start; i < end; ++i) {
276	    const int64_t bits = rray_dbl_bits(v_x[i]);
277	    const int64_t key = rray_bits_is_nan(bits) ? INT64_MIN : rray_dbl_key(bits);
278	    out_key = out_key < key ? key : out_key;
279	  }

281	  return rray_bits_dbl(rray_dbl_key(out_key));

283	
284	static inline double rray_max_dbl_run(
285	  const double* v_x,
286	  r_ssize start,
287	  r_ssize end,
288	  double out
289	) {
290	  const int64_t init_key = rray_dbl_key(rray_dbl_bits(-INFINITY));
291	
292	  while (start < end) {
293	    if (rray_dbl_classify(out) == RRAY_DBL_missing) {
294	      return out;
295	    }
296	
297	    const r_ssize block_end = end - start > 1024 ? start + 1024 : end;
298	
299	    int64_t block_key = init_key;
300	    bool any_nan = false;
301	
302	    for (r_ssize i = start; i < block_end; ++i) {
303	      const int64_t bits = rray_dbl_bits(v_x[i]);
304	      const bool is_nan = rray_bits_is_nan(bits);
305	      const int64_t key = is_nan ? INT64_MIN : rray_dbl_key(bits);
306	      block_key = block_key < key ? key : block_key;
307	      any_nan = bool_bitwise_or(any_nan, is_nan);
308	    }
309	
310	    if (any_nan) {
311	      out = rray_max_dbl_run_slow(v_x, start, block_end, out);
312	    } else {
313	      out = rray_max_dbl_one(out, rray_bits_dbl(rray_dbl_key(block_key)));
314	    }
315	
316	    start = block_end;
317	  }
318	
319	  return out;
320	}
321	
322	static double rray_max_dbl_run_slow(
323	  const double* v_x,
324	  r_ssize start,
325	  r_ssize end,
326	  double out
327	) {
328	  for (r_ssize i = start; i < end; ++i) {
329	    out = rray_max_dbl_one(out, v_x[i]);
330	  }
331	  return out;
332	}
333	
334	static inline int64_t rray_dbl_bits(double x) {
335	  int64_t out;
336	  memcpy(&out, &x, sizeof(out));
337	  return out;
338	}
339	
340	static inline double rray_bits_dbl(int64_t x) {
341	  double out;
342	  memcpy(&out, &x, sizeof(out));
343	  return out;
344	}
345	
346	static inline bool rray_bits_is_nan(int64_t x) {
347	  return (x & INT64_MAX) > 0x7ff0000000000000;
348	}
349	
350	static inline int64_t rray_dbl_key(int64_t x) {
351	  return x ^ ((x >> 63) & INT64_MAX);
352	}

+  struct rray_run_iterator it;
+  rray_run_iterator_init1(
+    &it,
+    v_dimensions,
+    dimensionality,
+    v_out_broadcast_strides
+  );
+
+  r_obj* out = KEEP(r_alloc_double(out_size));
+  double* v_out = r_dbl_begin(out);
+
+  for (r_ssize i = 0; i < out_size; ++i) {
+    v_out[i] = -INFINITY;
+  }
+
+  r_obj* missings = KEEP(r_alloc_raw0(out_size));
+  uint8_t* v_missings = r_raw_begin(missings);
+
+  const double* v_x = r_dbl_cbegin(x);
+
+  for (; !rray_run_iterator_done(&it); rray_run_iterator_next1(&it)) {
+    const r_ssize start = rray_run_iterator_start(&it);
+    const r_ssize end = rray_run_iterator_end(&it);
+
+    r_ssize out_loc = rray_run_iterator_loc(&it, 0);
+    const r_ssize out_stride = rray_run_iterator_stride(&it, 0);
+
+    if (out_stride == 0) {
+      double out_elt = v_out[out_loc];
+      uint8_t missing = v_missings[out_loc];
+
+      for (r_ssize i = start; i < end; ++i) {
+        const double x_elt = v_x[i];
+        const uint8_t x_missing = rray_dbl_classify(x_elt);
+        out_elt = rray_max_dbl_one_na_rm(out_elt, x_elt);
+        missing = missing < x_missing ? x_missing : missing;
+      }
+
+      v_out[out_loc] = out_elt;
+      v_missings[out_loc] = missing;
+    } else {
+      for (r_ssize i = start; i < end; ++i) {
+        const double x_elt = v_x[i];
+        const uint8_t x_missing = rray_dbl_classify(x_elt);
+        v_out[out_loc] = rray_max_dbl_one_na_rm(v_out[out_loc], x_elt);
+        v_missings[out_loc] =
+          v_missings[out_loc] < x_missing ? x_missing : v_missings[out_loc];
+        out_loc += out_stride;
+      }
+    }
+  }
+
+  for (r_ssize i = 0; i < out_size; ++i) {
+    switch (v_missings[i]) {
+    case RRAY_DBL_number:
+      break;
+    case RRAY_DBL_nan:
+      v_out[i] = R_NaN;
+      break;
+    case RRAY_DBL_missing:
+      v_out[i] = r_globals.na_dbl;
+      break;
+    }
+  }
+
+  FREE(2);
+  return out;
+}

I built it without the bit tricks. It gives the same results as main, including the sign of zero. It mostly helps na_rm = FALSE: the "over 2" case is 2.5x faster and the stride 0 cases are about 20% faster. One case is 2x slower, explained below.

The design. It follows sum: v_missings is one uint8_t per output. In both branches the main loop calls the existing rray_max_dbl_one_na_rm(), which already skips NaN because comparisons with NaN are false. It records the worst kind of missing value seen with rray_dbl_classify(). A fix-up loop then writes NaN or NA. I reordered enum rray_dbl_class to number, nan, missing so that a plain max gives NA precedence over NaN. na_rm = TRUE goes back to RRAY_REDUCE. All the bit helpers and the block code are gone.

Correctness:

  • The reduce and extremum tests pass (647 pass, 0 fail).

  • Against base R's max() on the 5,600 random cases (including long runs), there are no differences at all, not even in the sign of zero.

Benchmark, na_rm = FALSE (ms, fastest of 3 rounds):

Shape missing main branch ratio
[4000, 4000] over 1 none 22.54 17.98 0.80
[4000, 4000] over 2 none 13.60 5.46 0.40
[4000, 4000] over 1, 2 none 22.53 18.02 0.80
[200, 200, 200] over 1, 3 none 11.15 8.53 0.77
[10, 1e6] over 1 none 10.04 7.65 0.76
[4000, 4000] over 1, 2 sparse 9.13 18.02 1.97

The other sparse rows match the "none" rows.

What changed and why:

  • Over 2 (0.40): the branchy classify switch in the hot loop is gone, so the loop is now just a compare and select plus a small max.

  • Stride 0 (about 0.80): the loop is still a scalar fcmp/fcsel chain, one element at a time. Without the bit trick, clang won't vectorize a double max unless -ffast-math is on. So this is about as fast as na_rm = TRUE already was (17.9 ms).

  • The one regression (1.97): a single 16M-element run with an NA near the start. Main gets fast there by accident: once the running value is NA, rray_max_dbl_one() returns at its first switch case, so the rest of the run takes the cheap path. The new loop takes the same time whatever the data, so it costs 18 ms here, just like data with no NA.

na_rm = TRUE: unchanged from main, since it uses RRAY_REDUCE again. The exception is [10, 1e6] over 1, at 0.64 (6.98 → 4.47 ms). That gain comes from the earlier RRAY_REDUCE local variable change, which pays off on short runs. My first benchmark didn't include that shape.

Bottom line: without bit tricks, the stride 0 double cases top out around 18 ms (from 22.5), compared with about 5 ms using the int64 key. The clear win from this design is the over 2 case.

No new r_obj* values need protecting: out and missings are both wrapped in KEEP(), and FREE(2) happens right before the return. Nothing is committed. Double min is untouched, and the int max/min prototype is still in place. Should I apply the same design to double min?

Right: the stride 0 loop is still scalar. The other branch is SIMD now, which is where the 0.40 came from.

From the assembly of the new rray_max_dbl():

  • Stride 0: one element at a time. The classify step no longer branches (cmp #1954 / cinc / csel), but the max is still an fcmp + fcsel chain where each step waits on the last.

  • The other branch: clang builds a SIMD version, with 1954 copied into a vector register, and guards it with a runtime check that out and x don't overlap. That works because each lane updates a different output, so no chain of waiting forms.

The stride 0 limit is clang's rule, not our code. clang will only turn a running double max into SIMD code if it may assume there are no NaNs and that -0 and 0 can be treated as equal. Those are -ffast-math flags, and clang has no pragma that turns them on for just one loop.

So there are only three ways to get SIMD on the stride 0 path:

  1. The int64 key. The bit trick you ruled out.

  2. Platform intrinsics. Hand-written NEON/SSE code, with one version per architecture. That's a lot more complicated.

  3. Four running values by hand. Still not SIMD, but four separate chains run side by side. On this chip that should land somewhere between today's 18 ms and the key's 5 ms. The cost is that the order changes, so a max over 0 and -0 can return a different zero.

If none of those are worth it, the current design is a reasonable place to stop. It's simple, it matches base R exactly, it's 2.5x faster over 2 and about 20% faster on stride 0. Its one weak spot is the early-NA case where main was fast by accident.

Davis

Explain int64 trick

Claude

The trick is to treat each double's 64 bits as a signed integer, after one small fix for negative numbers. Then integer comparison gives the same order as double comparison, and clang will vectorize an integer max without -ffast-math.

How a double is stored. It has 1 sign bit, then 11 exponent bits, then 52 fraction bits. Two facts follow from that layout:

  • For positive doubles, a bigger number always has bigger bits. Exponent bits sit above fraction bits, so comparing the bits as an integer compares the exponent first and then the fraction. So bits(1.0) < bits(2.0) < bits(Inf).

  • Negative doubles have the same bits as their positive twin, except for the sign bit. Read as a signed int64 they come out negative, which is correct, but in the wrong order: a bigger magnitude gives a bigger bit pattern, so -2.0 would compare as larger than -1.0.

The fix. For negative doubles, flip every bit except the sign bit. That reverses their order:

key = bits ^ ((bits >> 63) & INT64_MAX)

  • bits >> 63 is all ones for a negative number and all zeros otherwise. This is an arithmetic shift, which copies the sign bit.

  • & INT64_MAX keeps every bit except the sign.

  • ^ flips those bits for negatives and does nothing for positives.

Now plain int64 < gives the double order:

-Inf  <  -2.0  <  -1.0  <  -0.0  <  0.0  <  1.0  <  2.0  <  Inf

Going back. The key keeps the sign bit, so applying the same formula again undoes it. It's its own inverse.

Handling NaN. A NaN has every exponent bit set and a fraction that isn't zero, so (bits & INT64_MAX) > 0x7ff0000000000000 detects it. With na_rm = TRUE, swap its key for INT64_MIN so it never wins the max.

Why it vectorizes. Each step is a load, a few bit operations, an integer compare and a select. That's the same kind of max clang already vectorized for int. Integer max gives the same answer in any order, so clang is free to split the work into SIMD lanes. Floating point compare-select has no such guarantee without -ffast-math, because of NaN and signed zero.

Side effects:

  • -0.0 and 0.0 get different keys, so a max of the two always returns 0.0 and a min always returns -0.0. Main and base R return whichever came first.

  • To tell NA from NaN you still need the payload check, or the slow fallback I had before. That's what made the na_rm = FALSE version complicated.

Davis

What’s the difference between dbl bits and dbl key

Claude

rray_dbl_bits() only reinterprets the double's 64 bits as an int64_t and changes nothing. rray_dbl_key() then flips the bits of negative numbers so that integer order matches double order.

rray_dbl_bits(x) is a memcpy from a double into an int64_t. No bits change. Positive doubles already sort correctly as integers, but negative doubles sort backwards:

x        bits (hex)            as int64
-2.0     0xC000000000000000    -4611686018427387904
-1.0     0xBFF0000000000000    -4616189618054758400

As integers, -2.0 comes out larger than -1.0, which is wrong.

rray_dbl_key(bits) takes those bits and, for negative numbers, flips every bit except the sign:

x        key (hex)             as int64
-2.0     0xBFFFFFFFFFFFFFFF    -4611686018427387905
-1.0     0xC00FFFFFFFFFFFFF    -4607182418800017409

Now -2.0 comes out smaller, which is correct. Positive numbers pass through unchanged.

They were separate functions because the NaN check (rray_bits_is_nan()) needs the raw bits, and the max needs the key. In the loop it went double → bits → NaN check, then bits → key → compare. On the way out it went key → bits (the same flip again, since the flip undoes itself) → double (rray_bits_dbl()).

Davis

And that’s faster than using dbl classify on the double itself for NaN handling and one dbl key that does the reinterpret plus negative handling?

Claude

Write /tmp/key-test2.c 
#include <math.h>
#include <stdint.h>
#include <string.h>

static inline int64_t dbl_key(double x) {
  int64_t bits;
  memcpy(&bits, &x, sizeof(bits));
  return bits ^ ((bits >> 63) & INT64_MAX);
}

double key_max_na_rm(const double* v_x, long n, int64_t out_key) {
  for (long i = 0; i < n; ++i) {
    const double x = v_x[i];
    const int64_t key = isnan(x) ? INT64_MIN : dbl_key(x);
    out_key = out_key < key ? key : out_key;
  }
  return (double) out_key;
}

No, it isn't faster. Your version is simpler and compiles to slightly less code. I tested it in a standalone file: isnan(x) on the double, plus a single dbl_key() that does the reinterpret and the negative flip in one step:

static inline int64_t dbl_key(double x) {
  int64_t bits;
  memcpy(&bits, &x, sizeof(bits));
  return bits ^ ((bits >> 63) & INT64_MAX);
}

const int64_t key = isnan(x) ? INT64_MIN : dbl_key(x);
out_key = out_key < key ? key : out_key;

clang still vectorizes it. The NaN test becomes one fcmeq.2d (x == x is false only for NaN), compared with my integer mask-and-compare. The rest is the same cmlt/and/eor flip and the cmgt/bif max.

Splitting into rray_dbl_bits() and rray_dbl_key() never helped speed. Everything is inlined, and the memcpy costs nothing because the value is already in a register. I split them only to do the NaN test on raw bits, and isnan() makes that unnecessary.

rray_dbl_classify() also compiled without branches in the last v_missings prototype (cmp #1954 / cinc / csel). So you could combine the two ideas:

  • Keep the v_missings design as it is now, with rray_dbl_classify() tracking NA vs NaN, plus the fix-up loop.

  • Swap only the max for the key: isnan(x) ? INT64_MIN : rray_dbl_key(x).

That leaves one 3-line bit function and a key-to-double conversion at the end. It would likely make the stride 0 loop SIMD too. I haven't tried that combination in the package or benchmarked it.

Tests pass and the assembly has SIMD loops. Checking results against base R, then benchmarking.

Bash: awk '/^static r_obj\* rray_max_dbl\(/{f=1} /^static r_obj\* rray_min_lgl\(/{f=0} f' src/reduc… 
awk '/^static r_obj\* rray_max_dbl\(/{f=1} /^static r_obj\* rray_min_lgl\(/{f=0} f' src/reduce-extremum.c
static r_obj* rray_max_dbl(
  r_obj* x,
  bool na_rm,
  r_ssize out_size,
  const int* v_dimensions,
  int dimensionality,
  const r_ssize* v_out_broadcast_strides,
  struct r_lazy error_call
) {
  int n_prot = 0;

  r_obj* keys = KEEP_N(r_alloc_raw(out_size * sizeof(int64_t)), &n_prot);
  int64_t* v_keys = (int64_t*) r_raw_begin(keys);

  const int64_t init_key = rray_dbl_key(-INFINITY);

  for (r_ssize i = 0; i < out_size; ++i) {
    v_keys[i] = init_key;
  }

  uint8_t* v_missings = NULL;

  if (!na_rm) {
    r_obj* missings = KEEP_N(r_alloc_raw0(out_size), &n_prot);
    v_missings = (uint8_t*) r_raw_begin(missings);
  }

  struct rray_run_iterator it;
  rray_run_iterator_init1(
    &it,
    v_dimensions,
    dimensionality,
    v_out_broadcast_strides
  );

  const double* v_x = r_dbl_cbegin(x);

  if (na_rm) {
    for (; !rray_run_iterator_done(&it); rray_run_iterator_next1(&it)) {
      const r_ssize start = rray_run_iterator_start(&it);
      const r_ssize end = rray_run_iterator_end(&it);

      r_ssize out_loc = rray_run_iterator_loc(&it, 0);
      const r_ssize out_stride = rray_run_iterator_stride(&it, 0);

      if (out_stride == 0) {
        int64_t out_key = v_keys[out_loc];

        for (r_ssize i = start; i < end; ++i) {
          const double x_elt = v_x[i];
          const int64_t key = isnan(x_elt) ? INT64_MIN : rray_dbl_key(x_elt);
          out_key = out_key < key ? key : out_key;
        }

        v_keys[out_loc] = out_key;
      } else {
        for (r_ssize i = start; i < end; ++i) {
          const double x_elt = v_x[i];
          const int64_t key = isnan(x_elt) ? INT64_MIN : rray_dbl_key(x_elt);
          v_keys[out_loc] = v_keys[out_loc] < key ? key : v_keys[out_loc];
          out_loc += out_stride;
        }
      }
    }
  } else {
    for (; !rray_run_iterator_done(&it); rray_run_iterator_next1(&it)) {
      const r_ssize start = rray_run_iterator_start(&it);
      const r_ssize end = rray_run_iterator_end(&it);

      r_ssize out_loc = rray_run_iterator_loc(&it, 0);
      const r_ssize out_stride = rray_run_iterator_stride(&it, 0);

      if (out_stride == 0) {
        int64_t out_key = v_keys[out_loc];
        uint8_t missing = v_missings[out_loc];

        for (r_ssize i = start; i < end; ++i) {
          const double x_elt = v_x[i];
          const int64_t key = isnan(x_elt) ? INT64_MIN : rray_dbl_key(x_elt);
          const uint8_t x_missing = rray_dbl_classify(x_elt);
          out_key = out_key < key ? key : out_key;
          missing = missing < x_missing ? x_missing : missing;
        }

        v_keys[out_loc] = out_key;
        v_missings[out_loc] = missing;
      } else {
        for (r_ssize i = start; i < end; ++i) {
          const double x_elt = v_x[i];
          const int64_t key = isnan(x_elt) ? INT64_MIN : rray_dbl_key(x_elt);
          const uint8_t x_missing = rray_dbl_classify(x_elt);
          v_keys[out_loc] = v_keys[out_loc] < key ? key : v_keys[out_loc];
          v_missings[out_loc] =
            v_missings[out_loc] < x_missing ? x_missing : v_missings[out_loc];
          out_loc += out_stride;
        }
      }
    }
  }

  r_obj* out = KEEP_N(r_alloc_double(out_size), &n_prot);
  double* v_out = r_dbl_begin(out);

  if (na_rm) {
    for (r_ssize i = 0; i < out_size; ++i) {
      v_out[i] = rray_key_dbl(v_keys[i]);
    }
  } else {
    for (r_ssize i = 0; i < out_size; ++i) {
      switch (v_missings[i]) {
      case RRAY_DBL_number:
        v_out[i] = rray_key_dbl(v_keys[i]);
        break;
      case RRAY_DBL_nan:
        v_out[i] = R_NaN;
        break;
      case RRAY_DBL_missing:
        v_out[i] = r_globals.na_dbl;
        break;
      }
    }
  }

  FREE(n_prot);
  return out;
}

static inline int64_t rray_dbl_key(double x) {
  int64_t bits;
  memcpy(&bits, &x, sizeof(bits));
  return bits ^ ((bits >> 63) & INT64_MAX);
}

static inline double rray_key_dbl(int64_t key) {
  const int64_t bits = key ^ ((key >> 63) & INT64_MAX);
  double out;
  memcpy(&out, &bits, sizeof(out));
  return out;
}

The combination makes na_rm = TRUE 4 to 5x faster on stride 0, but it also makes some cases slower. With na_rm = FALSE, stride 0 only gets to about 0.58, and over 2 is worse than the plain-double design from before. Results still match base R's max() in all 5,600 random cases, apart from the expected sign-of-zero changes (181 of them, from long runs with many zeros). The reduce and extremum tests pass.

Here is rray_max_dbl() as it stands now:

static r_obj* rray_max_dbl(
  r_obj* x,
  bool na_rm,
  r_ssize out_size,
  const int* v_dimensions,
  int dimensionality,
  const r_ssize* v_out_broadcast_strides,
  struct r_lazy error_call
) {
  int n_prot = 0;

  r_obj* keys = KEEP_N(r_alloc_raw(out_size * sizeof(int64_t)), &n_prot);
  int64_t* v_keys = (int64_t*) r_raw_begin(keys);

  const int64_t init_key = rray_dbl_key(-INFINITY);

  for (r_ssize i = 0; i < out_size; ++i) {
    v_keys[i] = init_key;
  }

  uint8_t* v_missings = NULL;

  if (!na_rm) {
    r_obj* missings = KEEP_N(r_alloc_raw0(out_size), &n_prot);
    v_missings = (uint8_t*) r_raw_begin(missings);
  }

  struct rray_run_iterator it;
  rray_run_iterator_init1(
    &it,
    v_dimensions,
    dimensionality,
    v_out_broadcast_strides
  );

  const double* v_x = r_dbl_cbegin(x);

  if (na_rm) {
    for (; !rray_run_iterator_done(&it); rray_run_iterator_next1(&it)) {
      const r_ssize start = rray_run_iterator_start(&it);
      const r_ssize end = rray_run_iterator_end(&it);

      r_ssize out_loc = rray_run_iterator_loc(&it, 0);
      const r_ssize out_stride = rray_run_iterator_stride(&it, 0);

      if (out_stride == 0) {
        int64_t out_key = v_keys[out_loc];

        for (r_ssize i = start; i < end; ++i) {
          const double x_elt = v_x[i];
          const int64_t key = isnan(x_elt) ? INT64_MIN : rray_dbl_key(x_elt);
          out_key = out_key < key ? key : out_key;
        }

        v_keys[out_loc] = out_key;
      } else {
        for (r_ssize i = start; i < end; ++i) {
          const double x_elt = v_x[i];
          const int64_t key = isnan(x_elt) ? INT64_MIN : rray_dbl_key(x_elt);
          v_keys[out_loc] = v_keys[out_loc] < key ? key : v_keys[out_loc];
          out_loc += out_stride;
        }
      }
    }
  } else {
    for (; !rray_run_iterator_done(&it); rray_run_iterator_next1(&it)) {
      const r_ssize start = rray_run_iterator_start(&it);
      const r_ssize end = rray_run_iterator_end(&it);

      r_ssize out_loc = rray_run_iterator_loc(&it, 0);
      const r_ssize out_stride = rray_run_iterator_stride(&it, 0);

      if (out_stride == 0) {
        int64_t out_key = v_keys[out_loc];
        uint8_t missing = v_missings[out_loc];

        for (r_ssize i = start; i < end; ++i) {
          const double x_elt = v_x[i];
          const int64_t key = isnan(x_elt) ? INT64_MIN : rray_dbl_key(x_elt);
          const uint8_t x_missing = rray_dbl_classify(x_elt);
          out_key = out_key < key ? key : out_key;
          missing = missing < x_missing ? x_missing : missing;
        }

        v_keys[out_loc] = out_key;
        v_missings[out_loc] = missing;
      } else {
        for (r_ssize i = start; i < end; ++i) {
          const double x_elt = v_x[i];
          const int64_t key = isnan(x_elt) ? INT64_MIN : rray_dbl_key(x_elt);
          const uint8_t x_missing = rray_dbl_classify(x_elt);
          v_keys[out_loc] = v_keys[out_loc] < key ? key : v_keys[out_loc];
          v_missings[out_loc] =
            v_missings[out_loc] < x_missing ? x_missing : v_missings[out_loc];
          out_loc += out_stride;
        }
      }
    }
  }

  r_obj* out = KEEP_N(r_alloc_double(out_size), &n_prot);
  double* v_out = r_dbl_begin(out);

  if (na_rm) {
    for (r_ssize i = 0; i < out_size; ++i) {
      v_out[i] = rray_key_dbl(v_keys[i]);
    }
  } else {
    for (r_ssize i = 0; i < out_size; ++i) {
      switch (v_missings[i]) {
      case RRAY_DBL_number:
        v_out[i] = rray_key_dbl(v_keys[i]);
        break;
      case RRAY_DBL_nan:
        v_out[i] = R_NaN;
        break;
      case RRAY_DBL_missing:
        v_out[i] = r_globals.na_dbl;
        break;
      }
    }
  }

  FREE(n_prot);
  return out;
}

static inline int64_t rray_dbl_key(double x) {
  int64_t bits;
  memcpy(&bits, &x, sizeof(bits));
  return bits ^ ((bits >> 63) & INT64_MAX);
}

static inline double rray_key_dbl(int64_t key) {
  const int64_t bits = key ^ ((key >> 63) & INT64_MAX);
  double out;
  memcpy(&out, &bits, sizeof(out));
  return out;
}

The enum reorder in src/missing.h (number, nan, missing) and the two helper declarations in the decl header are still in place.

Results (ms, fastest of 3 rounds; "Previous" is the plain-double v_missings design):

na_rm Shape main Previous Combo ratio vs main
TRUE [4000, 4000] over 1 17.88 17.88 4.01 0.22
TRUE [200, 200, 200] over 1, 3 7.55 7.56 2.05 0.27
TRUE [4000, 4000] over 2 2.39 2.26 4.05 1.69
FALSE [4000, 4000] over 1 22.54 17.98 13.00 0.58
FALSE [4000, 4000] over 2 13.60 5.46 10.84 0.80
FALSE [10, 1e6] over 1 10.11 7.65 10.75 1.06
FALSE, sparse NA [4000, 4000] over 1, 2 9.14 18.02 12.96 1.42

What I think is happening:

  • The key only pays off on stride 0. In the other branch, the double compare-select was already SIMD. The key adds a few more steps per element, so na_rm = TRUE over 2 got 1.7x slower. There's also an extra pass at the end to turn keys back into doubles.

  • na_rm = FALSE on stride 0 stops at 13 ms, not 4. The only difference from the na_rm = TRUE loop is the rray_dbl_classify() call, so that's the likely limit. I haven't checked that loop's assembly yet.

What I'd try next: use the key only inside the stride 0 loops. Convert out_elt to a key at the start of each run and back at the end. The other branch would stay on plain doubles with rray_max_dbl_one_na_rm(). That removes the key buffer and the final conversion pass, and should keep over 2 at the earlier speed (2.3 ms and 5.5 ms). For the remaining na_rm = FALSE gap on stride 0, I'd look at the assembly to see whether the classify step is what blocks SIMD.

No new r_obj* values need protecting: keys, missings and out are each wrapped in KEEP_N() as they are allocated, and FREE(n_prot) happens right before the return. Nothing is committed. Want me to try the stride-0-only version?

Davis

Investigate the classify step

Claude
